name="description" content="Familial hypercholesterolemia (FH) is characterized by significantly elevated low-density lipoprotein cholesterol (LDL-C) that leads to atherosclerotic plaque deposition in the coronary arteries and proximal aorta at an early age and increases the risk of premature cardiovascular events such as angina and myocardial infarction; stroke occurs more rarely. Xanthomas (cholesterol deposits in tendons) may be visible in the Achilles tendons or tendons of the hands and worsen with age as a result of extremely high cholesterol levels. Xanthelasmas (yellowish, waxy deposits) can occur around the eyelids. Individuals with FH may develop corneal arcus (white, gray, or blue opaque ring in the corneal margin as a result of cholesterol deposition) at a younger age than those without FH." /><meta name="og:title" content="Familial Hypercholesterolemia" /><meta name="og:type" content="book" /><meta name="og:description" content="Familial hypercholesterolemia (FH) is characterized by significantly elevated low-density lipoprotein cholesterol (LDL-C) that leads to atherosclerotic plaque deposition in the coronary arteries and proximal aorta at an early age and increases the risk of premature cardiovascular events such as angina and myocardial infarction; stroke occurs more rarely. Xanthomas (cholesterol deposits in tendons) may be visible in the Achilles tendons or tendons of the hands and worsen with age as a result of extremely high cholesterol levels. Xanthelasmas (yellowish, waxy deposits) can occur around the eyelids. Individuals with FH may develop corneal arcus (white, gray, or blue opaque ring in the corneal margin as a result of cholesterol deposition) at a younger age than those without FH." /><meta             <div class="main-content lit-style" itemscope="itemscope" itemtype="http://schema.org/CreativeWork"><div class="meta-content fm-sec"><h1 id="_NBK174884_"><span class="title" itemprop="name">Familial Hypercholesterolemia</span></h1><div itemprop="alternativeHeadline" class="subtitle whole_rhythm">Synonyms: Familial Hypercholesterol&#x000e6;mia, Hyperlipoproteinemia Type IIA</div><p class="contrib-group"><span itemprop="author">Hannah E Ison</span>, MS, LCGC, <span itemprop="author">Shoa L Clarke</span>, MD, PhD, and <span itemprop="author">Joshua W Knowles</span>, MD, PhD.</p><a data-jig="ncbitoggler" href="#__NBK174884_ai__" style="border:0;text-decoration:none">Author Information and Affiliations</a><div style="display:none" class="ui-widget" id="__NBK174884_ai__"><div class="contrib half_rhythm"><span itemprop="author">Hannah E Ison</span>, MS, LCGC<div class="affiliation small">Stanford Center for Inherited Cardiovascular Disease<br />Stanford, California<div><span class="email-label">Email: </span><a href="mailto:dev@null" data-email="gro.erachtlaehdrofnats@nosih" class="oemail">gro.erachtlaehdrofnats@nosih</a></div></div></div><div class="contrib half_rhythm"><span itemprop="author">Shoa L Clarke</span>, MD, PhD<div class="affiliation small">Department of Medicine, Division of Cardiovascular Medicine;<br />Department of Pediatrics, Division of Pediatric Cardiology<br />Stanford University School of Medicine<br />Stanford, California<div><span class="email-label">Email: </span><a href="mailto:dev@null" data-email="ude.drofnats@aohs" class="oemail">ude.drofnats@aohs</a></div></div></div><div class="contrib half_rhythm"><span itemprop="author">Joshua W Knowles</span>, MD, PhD<div class="affiliation small">Stanford Center for Inherited Cardiovascular Disease<br />Stanford, California<div><span class="email-label">Email: </span><a href="mailto:dev@null" data-email="ude.drofnats@jelwonk" class="oemail">ude.drofnats@jelwonk</a></div></div></div></div><p class="small">Initial Posting: <span itemprop="datePublished">January 2, 2014</span>; Last Revision: <span itemprop="dateModified">January 30, 2025</span>.</p><p><em>Estimated reading time: 49 minutes</em></p></div><div class="jig-ncbiinpagenav body-content whole_rhythm" data-jigconfig="allHeadingLevels: ['h2'],smoothScroll: false" itemprop="text"><div id="hyperchol.Summary" itemprop="description"><h2 id="_hyperchol_Summary_">Summary</h2><div><h4 class="inline">Clinical characteristics.</h4><p>Familial hypercholesterolemia (FH) is characterized by significantly elevated low-density lipoprotein cholesterol (LDL-C) that leads to atherosclerotic plaque deposition in the coronary arteries and proximal aorta at an early age and increases the risk of premature cardiovascular events such as angina and myocardial infarction; stroke occurs more rarely. Xanthomas (cholesterol deposits in tendons) may be visible in the Achilles tendons or tendons of the hands and worsen with age as a result of extremely high cholesterol levels. Xanthelasmas (yellowish, waxy deposits) can occur around the eyelids. Individuals with FH may develop corneal arcus (white, gray, or blue opaque ring in the corneal margin as a result of cholesterol deposition) at a younger age than those without FH.</p><p>Individuals with a more severe <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/phenotype/">phenotype</a>, often as a result of <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/biallelic/">biallelic</a> variants, can present with very significant elevations in LDL-C (&#x0003e;500 mg/dL), early-onset coronary artery disease (CAD; presenting as early as childhood in some), and calcific aortic valve disease.</p></div><div><h4 class="inline">Diagnosis/testing.</h4><p>A clinical diagnosis of FH can be established in a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a> with characteristic clinical features and significantly elevated LDL-C levels (typically &#x0003e;190 mg/dL in adults and &#x0003e;160 mg/dL in children). Three formal diagnostic criteria are used in Western countries.</p><p>The molecular diagnosis of FH can be established by identification of <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heterozygous/">heterozygous</a> or <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/biallelic/">biallelic</a> pathogenic variants in <i>APOB</i> (variants that impair binding of LDL-C to the LDL receptor), <i>LDLR</i>, or <i>PCSK9</i> (gain of function); or rarely, identification of biallelic pathogenic variants in <i>LDLRAP1</i>.</p></div><div><h4 class="inline">Management.</h4><p><i>Treatment of manifestations:</i> Adults: pharmacotherapy (statins with additional medications as needed) to reduce lipid levels; referral to a lipid specialist if necessary to reduce LDL-C levels; reduce CAD risk factors including cessation of smoking, regular physical activity, healthy diet, and weight control; treatment of hypertension; low-dose aspirin in high-risk individuals. Children: referral to a lipid specialist; diet and lifestyle modifications; statins can be used in children starting around age eight years.</p><p><i>Prevention of primary manifestations:</i> Heart-healthy diet (including reduced intake of saturated fat and increased intake of soluble fiber to 10-20 g/day); increased physical activity; no smoking.</p><p><i>Surveillance:</i> Monitor lipid levels from age two years; consider noninvasive imaging modalities in adults; identify modifiable risk factors (e.g., smoking, sedentary behavior, hypertension, diabetes, obesity). Individuals with severe FH (i.e., due to <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/homozygous/">homozygous</a> or <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/compound-heterozygous/">compound heterozygous</a> pathogenic variants in <i>APOB</i>, <i>LDLR</i>, or <i>PCSK9</i>) or <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/autosomal-recessive/">autosomal recessive</a> FH (due to homozygous or compound heterozygous pathogenic variants in <i>LDLRAP1</i>) should be monitored with various imaging modalities (including echocardiogram, CT angiogram, and cardiac catheterization) as recommended.</p><p><i>Agents/circumstances to avoid:</i> Smoking, high intake of saturated and trans unsaturated fat, sedentary lifestyle, obesity, hypertension, and diabetes mellitus.</p><p><i>Evaluation of relatives at risk:</i> Early diagnosis and treatment of first-degree and second-degree relatives at risk for FH can reduce morbidity and mortality. The genetic status of at-risk family members can be clarified by either: (1) <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/molecular-genetic-testing/">molecular genetic testing</a> if the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a>(s) has been identified in an affected family member; or (2) measurement of LDL-C concentration. Genetic testing is the preferred method for clarifying the diagnosis in at-risk family members, when possible.</p><p><i>Pregnancy management:</i> Pregnant women should incorporate all the recommended lifestyle changes, including low saturated fat intake, no smoking, and high dietary soluble fiber. Statins are contraindicated in pregnancy because of concerns for teratogenicity and should be discontinued prior to conception. Bile acid-binding resins (e.g., colesevelam) are generally considered safe (Class B for pregnancy), and LDL apheresis is also used occasionally if there is evidence of established CAD. Use of PCSK9 inhibitors, ezetimibe, lomitapide, and bempedoic acid during pregnancy has not been well studied.</p></div><div><h4 class="inline">Genetic counseling.</h4><p><i>APOB</i>-, <i>LDLR</i>-, and <i>PCSK9</i>-related FH are inherited in an <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/autosomal-dominant/">autosomal dominant</a> manner. If an individual has <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/biallelic/">biallelic</a> (<a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/homozygous/">homozygous</a> or <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/compound-heterozygous/">compound heterozygous</a>) pathogenic variants in one of these three genes &#x02013; a condition referred to as homozygous FH (HoFH) &#x02013; the presentation becomes more severe with earlier onset of features. Some individuals with FH are heterozygous for pathogenic variants in two different FH-related genes, which may have an additive effect on the severity of FH. Each child of an individual with a heterozygous <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> in <i>APOB</i>, <i>LDLR</i>, or <i>PCSK9</i> has a 50% chance of inheriting the pathogenic variant and having FH. All children of an individual with homozygous FH will inherit a pathogenic variant and have FH. If the reproductive partner of a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a> is heterozygous for an FH-related pathogenic variant in the same <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/gene/">gene</a> as the proband or a different FH-related gene, offspring are at risk of inheriting two pathogenic variants and having severe FH.</p><p><i>LDLRAP1</i>-related FH is caused by <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/biallelic/">biallelic</a> pathogenic variants and is inherited in an <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/autosomal-recessive/">autosomal recessive</a> manner. The parents of an individual with <i>LDLRAP1</i>-related FH are presumed to be <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heterozygous/">heterozygous</a> for one <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a>. If both parents are known to be heterozygous for an <i>LDLRAP1</i> pathogenic variant, each sib of an affected individual has at conception a 25% chance of being affected, a 50% chance of being a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/carrier/">carrier</a>, and a 25% chance of inheriting neither of the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/familial/">familial</a> pathogenic variants. Carrier testing for at-risk relatives requires prior identification of the <i>LDLRAP1</i> pathogenic variants in the family.</p><p>Once the FH-causing <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a>(s) have been identified in an affected family member, prenatal and <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/preimplantation-genetic-testing/">preimplantation genetic testing</a> are possible.</p></div></div><div id="hyperchol.Diagnosis"><h2 id="_hyperchol_Diagnosis_">Diagnosis</h2><div id="hyperchol.Suggestive_Findings"><h3>Suggestive Findings</h3><p>Familial hypercholesterolemia (FH) should be suspected in individuals with the following findings.</p><p>
<b>Extreme hypercholesterolemia</b>
</p><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Adults (untreated):</div><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Low-density lipoprotein cholesterol (LDL-C) levels &#x0003e;190 mg/dL (&#x0003e;4.9 mmol/L)</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Total cholesterol levels &#x0003e;310 mg/dL (&#x0003e;8 mmol/L)</div></li></ul></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Children/adolescents (untreated):</div><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>LDL-C levels &#x0003e;190 mg/dL (&#x02265;4.9 mmol/L) [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.wiegman.2015.2425">Wiegman et al 2015</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.de_ferranti.2019.e603">de Ferranti et al 2019</a>]</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>LDL-C levels &#x0003e;160 mg/dL, particularly when there is a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/first-degree-relative/">first-degree relative</a> with hyperlipidemia and/or premature coronary artery disease (onset in males age &#x02264;55 years and females &#x02264;65 years) [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.wiegman.2015.2425">Wiegman et al 2015</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.sturm.2018.662">Sturm at al 2018</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.de_ferranti.2019.e603">de Ferranti et al 2019</a>]</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>LDL-C levels &#x0003e;130 mg/dL (&#x0003e;3.4 mmol/L) in those with a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/first-degree-relative/">first-degree relative</a> with FH [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.wiegman.2015.2425">Wiegman et al 2015</a>]</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Total cholesterol levels &#x0003e;230 mg/dL (&#x0003e;6 mmol/L)</div></li></ul></li></ul><p><b>History of premature coronary artery disease</b> (CAD) such as myocardial infarction or obstructive CAD requiring intervention or other cardiovascular disease (e.g., ischemic stroke, peripheral vascular disease). Premature CAD is typically defined as disease identified before age 55 years in males and before age 65 years in females.</p><p>
<b>Physical examination findings</b>
</p><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div class="half_rhythm">Xanthomas (cholesterol deposits in tendons) may be identified in adults but are unlikely to present in children with <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heterozygous/">heterozygous</a> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/familial/">familial</a> hypercholesterolemia (HeFH).</div><div class="half_rhythm">Note: Xanthomas can present within the first few years of life in those with a more severe <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/phenotype/">phenotype</a> due to <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/biallelic/">biallelic</a> variants [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.wiegman.2015.2425">Wiegman et al 2015</a>].</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div class="half_rhythm">Xanthelasmas (yellowish, waxy deposits that can occur around the eyelids)</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div class="half_rhythm">Corneal arcus (white, gray, or blue opaque ring in the corneal margin as a result of cholesterol deposition) is more likely to be FH-related when present in an individual younger than age 45 years.</div></li></ul><p><b>Family history</b> of any of the following:</p><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>FH</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Elevated LDL-C levels</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Premature CAD</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Xanthomas/xanthelasmas</div></li></ul><p>Note: (1) Age-specific LDL-C or total cholesterol levels are more specific in determining the likelihood of FH (e.g., &#x0003e;95th percentile for age, sex, and country) [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.nordestgaard.2013.3478">Nordestgaard et al 2013</a>]. (2) Electronic applications (see <a href="http://www.appdropp.com/ios/fh-diagnosis/543676258" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">FH Diagnosis</a>) can assist with the determination of the likelihood of FH based on the formal diagnostic criteria.</p></div><div id="hyperchol.Establishing_the_Diagnosis"><h3>Establishing the Diagnosis</h3><p>The <b>clinical diagnosis</b> of FH can be <b>established</b> in a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a> with features described in <a href="#hyperchol.Suggestive_Findings">Suggestive Findings</a>. Currently <a href="https://thefhfoundation.org/diagnostic-criteria-for-familia-hypercholesterolemia" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">three formal diagnostic criteria</a> for FH are widely used in Western countries: the MEDPED Criteria, the Simon Broome Criteria, and the FH Dutch Lipid Clinic Network Criteria. (See <a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.haradashiba.2012.1043">Harada-Shiba et al [2012]</a> for criteria used in non-Western countries.) All use various FH-related features to establish the diagnosis (e.g., LDL-C levels, history of CAD, physical examination findings, family history); LDL-C levels are incorporated into all three criteria.</p><p>Note: The criteria used to establish the clinical diagnosis of FH in children varies substantially across medical centers within and outside of the United States. Some experts recommend the Simon Broome criteria in pediatric probands rather than the Dutch Lipid Clinic Network Criteria [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.wiegman.2015.2425">Wiegman et al 2015</a>].</p><p>A <b>molecular diagnosis</b> of FH can be <b>established</b> in a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a> by identification of:</p><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div class="half_rhythm">A <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heterozygous/">heterozygous</a> pathogenic (or <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/likely-pathogenic/">likely pathogenic</a>) variant in <i>LDLR</i></div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div class="half_rhythm">A <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heterozygous/">heterozygous</a> pathogenic (or <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/likely-pathogenic/">likely pathogenic</a>) variant in <i>APOB</i> (impairing binding of LDL-C to the LDL receptor)</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div class="half_rhythm">A <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heterozygous/">heterozygous</a> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/gain-of-function/">gain-of-function</a> pathogenic (or <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/likely-pathogenic/">likely pathogenic</a>) variant in <i>PCSK9</i></div><div class="half_rhythm">Note: A whole-<a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/gene/">gene</a> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/duplication/">duplication</a> of <i>PCSK9</i> has also been described in individuals with severe FH [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.iacocca.2018.1316">Iacocca et al 2018</a>].</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div class="half_rhythm">Biallelic <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/loss-of-function/">loss-of-function</a> pathogenic (or <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/likely-pathogenic/">likely pathogenic</a>) variants in <i>APOB</i>, <i>LDLR</i>, <i>LDLRAP1</i>, or <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/biallelic/">biallelic</a> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/gain-of-function/">gain-of-function</a> pathogenic (or likely pathogenic) variants in <i>PCSK9</i> (associated with severe FH).</div></li></ul><p>Note: (1) Per ACMG/AMP variant interpretation guidelines, the terms "pathogenic variants" and "<a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/likely-pathogenic/">likely pathogenic</a> variants" are synonymous in a clinical setting, meaning that both are considered diagnostic and can be used for clinical decision making [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.richards.2015.405">Richards et al 2015</a>]. Reference to "pathogenic variants" in this <i>GeneReview</i> is understood to include likely pathogenic variants. (2) Identification of variant(s) of <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/uncertain-significance/">uncertain significance</a> cannot be used to confirm or rule out the diagnosis.</p><p>Molecular genetic testing approaches can include a <b>smaller FH <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/multigene-panel/">multigene panel</a></b> or a <b>broader dyslipidemia multigene panel</b> depending on the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/phenotype/">phenotype</a>:</p><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div class="half_rhythm">A <b>smaller FH <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/multigene-panel/">multigene panel</a></b> that includes <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/sequence-analysis/">sequence analysis</a> and <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/deletion-duplication-analysis/">deletion/duplication analysis</a> of <i>APOB</i>, <i>LDLR</i>, <i>LDLRAP1</i>, and <i>PCSK9</i> may be considered.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div class="half_rhythm">A <b>broader dyslipidemia <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/multigene-panel/">multigene panel</a></b> that includes <i>APOB</i>, <i>LDLR</i>, <i>LDLRAP1</i>, and <i>PCSK9</i> as well as <i>ABCG8</i>, <i>ABCG5</i>, <i>APOE</i>, and <i>LIPA</i> (genes associated with other lipid conditions that can cause elevated LDL-C levels and early-onset CAD; see <a href="#hyperchol.Differential_Diagnosis">Differential Diagnosis</a>) may also be considered. Note: (1) The genes included and the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/sensitivity/">sensitivity</a> of multigene panels vary by laboratory and are likely to change over time. (2) Some multigene panels may include genes not associated with the condition discussed in this GeneReview; thus, clinicians need to determine which multigene panel is most likely to identify the genetic cause of the condition while limiting identification of variants of <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/uncertain-significance/">uncertain significance</a> and pathogenic variants in genes that do not explain the underlying <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/phenotype/">phenotype</a>. (3) In some laboratories, panel options may include a custom laboratory-designed panel and/or custom phenotype-focused <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/exome/">exome</a> analysis that includes genes specified by the clinician. (4) Methods used in a panel may include <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/sequence-analysis/">sequence analysis</a>, <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/deletion-duplication-analysis/">deletion/duplication analysis</a>, and/or other non-sequencing-based tests.</div><div class="half_rhythm">For an introduction to multigene panels click <a href="/sites/books/n/gene/app5/#app5.Multigene_Panels">here</a>. More detailed information for clinicians ordering genetic tests can be found <a href="/sites/books/n/gene/app5/#app5.Multigene_Panels_FAQs">here</a>.</div></li></ul><div id="hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us" class="table"><h3><span class="label">Table 1. </span></h3><div class="caption"><p>Molecular Genetic Testing Used in Familial Hypercholesterolemia</p></div><p class="large-table-link" style="display:none"><span class="right"><a href="/sites/books/NBK174884/table/hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us/?report=objectonly" target="object">View in own window</a></span></p><div class="large_tbl" id="__hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us_lrgtbl__"><table class="no_bottom_margin"><thead><tr><th Variants&#x000a0;<sup>3</sup> Identified by Method</th></tr><tr><th headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us_1_1_1_3" id="hd_h_hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us_1_1_2_1" colspan="1" scope="colgroup" rowspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Sequence analysis&#x000a0;<sup>4,&#x000a0;5</sup></th><th style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Gene-targeted <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/deletion-duplication-analysis/">deletion/duplication analysis</a>&#x000a0;<sup>5,&#x000a0;6</sup></th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>APOB</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">~5%-10%&#x000a0;<sup>7</sup></td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us_1_1_1_3 hd_h_hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us_1_1_2_1" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">100%</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us_1_1_1_3 hd_h_hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us_1_1_2_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">None reported</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>LDLR</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">&#x0003e;50%&#x000a0;<sup>7</sup></td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us_1_1_1_3 hd_h_hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us_1_1_2_1" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">~85%&#x000a0;<sup>8</sup></td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us_1_1_1_3 hd_h_hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us_1_1_2_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">~15%</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>LDLRAP1</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">&#x0003c;1%&#x000a0;<sup>9</sup></td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us_1_1_1_3 hd_h_hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us_1_1_2_1" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">&#x0003e;80%</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us_1_1_1_3 hd_h_hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us_1_1_2_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">&#x0003c;20%</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>PCSK9</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">&#x0003c;1%&#x000a0;<sup>7</sup></td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us_1_1_1_3 hd_h_hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us_1_1_2_1" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">~98%</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us_1_1_1_3 hd_h_hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us_1_1_2_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">2 families&#x000a0;<sup>10</sup></td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Unknown</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">~40%&#x000a0;<sup>11</sup></td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us_1_1_1_3 hd_h_hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us_1_1_2_1 hd_h_hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us_1_1_2_2" colspan="2" rowspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">NA</td></tr></tbody></table></div><div><div><dl class="temp-labeled-list small"><dt></dt><dd><div><p class="no_margin">FH = <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/familial/">familial</a> hypercholesterolemia; NA = not applicable</p></div></dd><dt>1. </dt><dd><div id="hyperchol.TF.1.1"><p class="no_margin">Genes are listed in alphabetic order</p></div></dd><dt>2. </dt><dd><div id="hyperchol.TF.1.2"><p class="no_margin">See <a href="/sites/books/NBK174884/#hyperchol.molgen.TA">Table A. Genes and Databases</a> for <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/chromosome/">chromosome</a> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/locus/">locus</a> and protein.</p></div></dd><dt>3. </dt><dd><div id="hyperchol.TF.1.3"><p class="no_margin">See <a href="#hyperchol.Molecular_Genetics">Molecular Genetics</a> for information on variants detected in this <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/gene/">gene</a>.</p></div></dd><dt>4. </dt><dd><div id="hyperchol.TF.1.4"><p class="no_margin">Sequence analysis detects variants that are benign, <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/likely-benign/">likely benign</a>, of <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/uncertain-significance/">uncertain significance</a>, <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/likely-pathogenic/">likely pathogenic</a>, or pathogenic. Variants may include <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/missense/">missense</a>, <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/nonsense-variant/">nonsense</a>, and <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/splice-site/">splice site</a> variants and small intragenic deletions/insertions; typically, <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/exon/">exon</a> or whole-<a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/gene/">gene</a> deletions/duplications are not detected. For issues to consider in interpretation of <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/sequence-analysis/">sequence analysis</a> results, click <a href="/sites/books/n/gene/app2/">here</a>.</p></div></dd><dt>5. </dt><dd><div id="hyperchol.TF.1.5"><p class="no_margin">Data derived from the subscription-based professional view of Human Gene Mutation Database [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.stenson.2020.1197">Stenson et al 2020</a>]</p></div></dd><dt>6. </dt><dd><div id="hyperchol.TF.1.6"><p class="no_margin">Gene-targeted <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/deletion-duplication-analysis/">deletion/duplication analysis</a> detects intragenic deletions or duplications. Methods used may include a range of techniques such as <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/quantitative-pcr/">quantitative PCR</a>, long-range PCR, multiplex ligation-dependent probe amplification (MLPA), and a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/gene/">gene</a>-targeted microarray designed to detect single-<a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/exon/">exon</a> deletions or duplications.</p></div></dd><dt>7. </dt><dd><div id="hyperchol.TF.1.7"><p class="no_margin">
<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.sturm.2018.662">Sturm et al [2018]</a>
</p></div></dd><dt>8. </dt><dd><div id="hyperchol.TF.1.8"><p class="no_margin">Sequence analysis of <i>LDLR</i> should include the regulatory region (200 bp upstream of the initiation codon).</p></div></dd><dt>9. </dt><dd><div id="hyperchol.TF.1.9"><p class="no_margin">To date, only ~30 <i>LDLRAP1</i> pathogenic variants have been identified, and most have been <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/biallelic/">biallelic</a> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/loss-of-function/">loss-of-function</a> variants [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.fahed.2011.23">Fahed &#x00026; Nemer 2011</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.cuchel.2014.2146">Cuchel et al 2014</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.hegele.2019.53">Hegele 2019</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.petrulioniene.2019.887">Petrulioniene et al 2019</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.derasmo.2020.56">D'Erasmo et al 2020</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.kamar.2021.572045">Kamar et al 2021</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.nikasa.2021.201">Nikasa et al 2021</a>].</p></div></dd><dt>10. </dt><dd><div id="hyperchol.TF.1.10"><p class="no_margin">Whole-<a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/gene/">gene</a> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/duplication/">duplication</a> was reported in two unrelated families with FH [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.iacocca.2018.1316">Iacocca et al 2018</a>].</p></div></dd><dt>11. </dt><dd><div id="hyperchol.TF.1.11"><p class="no_margin">FH disease-causing <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a>(s) can be identified in &#x0223c;60%-80% of adult probands with a clinical diagnosis of FH. FH disease-causing pathogenic variant(s) can be identified in &#x0223c;60%-95% of pediatric probands in whom there is a strong clinical suspicion of FH [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.sturm.2018.662">Sturm et al 2018</a>]. Therefore, in ~40% of individuals with a clinical diagnosis of FH, a genetic cause cannot be identified.</p></div></dd></dl></div></div></div></div></div><div id="hyperchol.Clinical_Characteristics"><h2 id="_hyperchol_Clinical_Characteristics_">Clinical Characteristics</h2><div id="hyperchol.Clinical_Description"><h3>Clinical Description</h3><div id="hyperchol.Heterozygous_Familial_Hypercho"><h4>Heterozygous Familial Hypercholesterolemia (FH; HeFH)</h4><p>Elevated low-density lipoprotein cholesterol (LDL-C) leads to atherosclerotic plaque deposition in the coronary arteries and other arterial beds starting at an early age and worsening over time. Individuals with FH have elevated LDL-C levels starting soon after birth. When left untreated, this can lead to an increased risk of angina, myocardial infarction, peripheral artery disease, and potentially stroke [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.scientific_steering_committee.1991.893">Scientific Steering Committee 1991</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.versmissen.2008.a2423">Versmissen et al 2008</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.elis.2011.223">Elis et al 2011</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.raal.2012.262">Raal &#x00026; Santos 2012</a>]. Individuals with an LDL-C greater than 190 mg/dL (&#x0003e;4.9 mmol/L) and a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> in one of the genes listed in <a href="/sites/books/NBK174884/table/hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobhypercholTmoleculargenetictestingus">Table 1</a> have a 22-fold increased risk for coronary artery disease (CAD) over the general population, while those without a pathogenic variant have a sixfold increased risk for CAD over the general population [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.khera.2016.2578">Khera et al 2016</a>]. Natural history studies from the pre-statin era suggest that untreated men are at 50% risk for a fatal or nonfatal coronary event by age 50 years; untreated women are at 30% risk by age 60 years [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.slack.1969.1380">Slack 1969</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.stone.1974.476">Stone et al 1974</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.civeira.2004.55">Civeira 2004</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.goldberg.2011.s1">Goldberg et al 2011</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.reiner.2011.1769">Reiner et al 2011</a>] (<a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK174884/figure/hyperchol.F1/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="fighypercholF1" rid-ob="figobhypercholF1">Figure 1</a>). Additional studies have found that approximately one in ten individuals with a history of early-onset myocardial infarction have FH, and this risk increased in the setting of a family history of CAD [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.singh.2019.2439">Singh et al 2019</a>]. Of note, standard Framingham or other risk classification schemes are not applicable to persons with FH [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.goldberg.2011.s1">Goldberg et al 2011</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.reiner.2011.1769">Reiner et al 2011</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.nordestgaard.2013.3478">Nordestgaard et al 2013</a>]. The risk of stroke in individuals with FH is less clear, with some studies finding that this risk may not be as strongly correlated with FH as initially thought [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.akioyamen.2019.726">Akioyamen et al 2019</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.hovland.2019">Hovland et al 2019</a>].</p><div class="iconblock whole_rhythm clearfix ten_col fig" id="fighypercholF1" co-legend-rid="figlgndhypercholF1"><a href="/sites/books/NBK174884/figure/hyperchol.F1/?report=objectonly" target="object" title="Figure 1. " class="img_link icnblk_img figpopup" rid-figpopup="fighypercholF1" rid-ob="figobhypercholF1"><img class="small-thumb" src="/sites/books/NBK174884/bin/hyperchol-Image001.gif" src-large="/sites/books/NBK174884/bin/hyperchol-Image001.jpg" alt="Figure 1. " /></a><div class="icnblk_cntnt" id="figlgndhypercholF1"><h4 id="hyperchol.F1"><a href="/sites/books/NBK174884/figure/hyperchol.F1/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobhypercholF1">Figure 1. </a></h4><p class="float-caption no_bottom_margin">LDL cholesterol burden in individuals with or without familial hypercholesterolemia as a function of the age of initiation of statin therapy Data derived from Starr et al [2008] and Huijgen et al [2012]. Figure from Nordestgaard et al [2013]; used by <a href="/sites/books/NBK174884/figure/hyperchol.F1/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobhypercholF1">(more...)</a></p></div></div><p>Lipid-lowering therapy with statin-based regimens (see Management, <a href="#hyperchol.Treatment_of_Manifestations">Treatment of Manifestations</a>) significantly increases survival [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.nordestgaard.2013.3478">Nordestgaard et al 2013</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.vuorio.2013.315">Vuorio et al 2013</a>] and reduces morbidity [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.versmissen.2008.a2423">Versmissen et al 2008</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.elis.2011.223">Elis et al 2011</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.braamskamp.2016.455">Braamskamp et al 2016</a>]. Additional medications such as ezetimibe, bile acid-binding resins, PCSK9 inhibiters, or bempedoic acid are often necessary to achieve optimal LDL-C reduction [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.gidding.2015.2167">Gidding et al 2015</a>]. LDL-C levels are typically unable to be lowered with lifestyle intervention alone.</p><p>Xanthomas represent cholesterol buildup in tendons of the body as a result of extremely high levels of LDL-C. Xanthomas may worsen with age in untreated persons. In persons treated with LDL-C-lowering therapy, the xanthomas can become smaller. Common locations:</p><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Tendonous xanthomas can occur in the elbows, hands, knees, and feet, particularly the Achilles tendon [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.tsouli.2005.236">Tsouli et al 2005</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.elis.2011.223">Elis et al 2011</a>]. These are historically described in 30%-50% of persons with FH, although more recent studies show a lower prevalence, likely because of widespread statin use [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.perez_de_isla.2016.1278">Perez de Isla et al 2016</a>].</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Interdigital xanthomas (between the fingers) occur in individuals with <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/biallelic/">biallelic</a> pathogenic variants in <i>APOB</i>, <i>LDLR</i>, or <i>PCSK9</i>.</div></li></ul><p>Xanthelasmas are patches of yellowish cholesterol deposits that often occur around the eyes [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.dey.2013.932863">Dey et al 2013</a>].</p><p>Corneal arcus (white, gray, or blue opaque ring in the corneal margin) is caused by abnormal deposition of lipids in the cornea secondary to long-term exposure to elevated LDL-C levels. This feature develops with age and is concerning for FH when identified before age 45 years. Studies have found that this feature may present in an estimated 7%-30% of individuals with FH [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.perez_de_isla.2016.1278">Perez de Isla et al 2016</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.rizos.2018.308">Rizos et al 2018</a>]. It is less likely to be identified in children with <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heterozygous/">heterozygous</a> FH-related pathogenic variants and in individuals who have been on lipid-lowering medications from a young age. This feature does not resolve with treatment.</p></div><div id="hyperchol.Homozygous_HoFH_and_Autosomal"><h4>Homozygous (HoFH) and Autosomal Recessive FH</h4><p>Individuals with <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/biallelic/">biallelic</a> pathogenic variants (either <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/homozygous/">homozygous</a> or <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/compound-heterozygous/">compound heterozygous</a> variants) in <i>APOB</i>, <i>LDLR</i>, <i>PCSK9</i>, or <i>LDLRAP1</i> can present with a more severe <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/phenotype/">phenotype</a> that varies by affected <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/gene/">gene</a> and residual enzyme function (see <a href="/sites/books/NBK174884/table/hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobhypercholTfamilialhypercholesterolemi">Table 2</a>) [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.mcgowan.2019.e013225">McGowan et al 2019</a>].</p></div></div><div id="hyperchol.Phenotype_Correlations_by_Gene"><h3>Phenotype Correlations by Gene</h3><div id="hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi" class="table"><h3><span class="label">Table 2. </span></h3><div class="caption"><p>Familial Hypercholesterolemia: Phenotype Correlations by Gene</p></div><p class="large-table-link" style="display:none"><span class="right"><a href="/sites/books/NBK174884/table/hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi/?report=objectonly" target="object">View in own window</a></span></p><div class="large_tbl" id="__hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_lrgtbl__"><table class="no_bottom_margin"><thead><tr><th id="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_1_1_1_1" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Gene</th><th id="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_1_1_1_2" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">MOI</th><th id="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_1_1_1_3" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Phenotype&#x000a0;<sup>1</sup></th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_1_1_1_1" rowspan="2" scope="row" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>APOB</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">AD</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Can be assoc w/&#x02193; LDL-C levels &#x00026; less severe presentation than <i>LDLR</i>-related HeFH</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_1_1_1_2" colspan="1" scope="row" rowspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">AR</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Rare, w/few persons reported. Presentation is reported to be more similar to <i>LDLR</i>-related HeFH, &#x00026; less severe than <i>LDLR</i>-related HoFH (e.g., &#x02193; LDL-C levels, later onset of CAD).</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_1_1_1_1" rowspan="2" scope="row" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>LDLR</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">AD</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">&#x02191; LDL-C often &#x0003e;190 mg/dL, &#x02191; risk of premature CAD; physical exam findings (e.g., xanthomas) may develop over time.</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_1_1_1_2" colspan="1" scope="row" rowspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">AR</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;"><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Untreated adults often have LDL-C levels &#x0003e;500 mg/dL (&#x0003e;13 mmol/L). In addition to xanthelasmas &#x00026; tendonous xanthomas, interdigital xanthomas (between fingers) can also occur &#x00026; develop in childhood. Most persons develop severe CAD by their mid-20s. The rate of either death or coronary bypass surgery by teen years is high [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.raal.2012.262">Raal &#x00026; Santos 2012</a>]. Severe aortic stenosis is also common [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.raal.2012.262">Raal &#x00026; Santos 2012</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.raal.2016a.238">Raal et al 2016a</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.raal.2016b.1458">Raal et al 2016b</a>].</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Statins are often relatively ineffective because their efficacy largely depends on upregulation of functional LDL receptors in liver [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.raal.2012.262">Raal &#x00026; Santos 2012</a>]; thus, therapy often requires LDL apheresis in addition to use of multiple other medications, some of which are specifically approved for HoFH, incl PCSK9 inhibitors, lomitapide, &#x00026; evinacumab (see Management, <a href="#hyperchol.Treatment_of_Manifestations">Treatment of Manifestations</a>).</div></li></ul>
</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>LDLRAP1</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">AR</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Presentation similar to <i>LDLR</i>-related HoFH incl LDL-C &#x0003e;500 mg/dL, early-onset CAD, &#x00026; aortic valve stenosis. Planar, tuberous, or tendon xanthomas have also been reported to develop in childhood. Persons have had variable response to lipid-lowering therapies, often requiring &#x0003e;1 &#x00026;/or requiring LDL apheresis.&#x000a0;<sup>2</sup></td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_1_1_1_1" rowspan="2" scope="row" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>PCSK9</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">AD</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;"><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Can be assoc w/&#x02193; LDL-C levels &#x00026; less severe presentation than <i>LDLR</i>-related HeFH</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Severe, early-onset FH has been reported in a few persons w/whole-<a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/gene/">gene</a> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/duplication/">duplication</a> [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.iacocca.2018.1316">Iacocca et al 2018</a>].</div></li></ul>
</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_1_1_1_2" colspan="1" scope="row" rowspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">AR</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Rare, w/few persons reported. Presentation reported to be more similar to that of <i>LDLR</i>-related HeFH &#x00026; less severe than <i>LDLR</i>-related HoFH (e.g., &#x02193; LDL-C levels, later onset of CAD).</td></tr></tbody></table></div><div><div><dl class="temp-labeled-list small"><dt></dt><dd><div><p class="no_margin">AD = <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/autosomal-dominant/">autosomal dominant</a>; AR = <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/autosomal-recessive/">autosomal recessive</a>; CAD = coronary artery disease; FH = <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/familial/">familial</a> hypercholesterolemia; HeFH = <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heterozygous/">heterozygous</a> familial hypercholesterolemia; HoFH = familial hypercholesterolemia due to <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/biallelic/">biallelic</a> (<a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/homozygous/">homozygous</a> or <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/compound-heterozygous/">compound heterozygous</a>) pathogenic variants in <i>APOB, LDLR</i>, or <i>PCSK9</i>; LDL-C = low-density lipoprotein cholesterol; MOI = <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/mode-of-inheritance/">mode of inheritance</a></p></div></dd><dt>1. </dt><dd><div id="hyperchol.TF.2.1"><p class="no_margin">
<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.moyer.2015.387">Moyer &#x00026; Baudhuin [2015]</a>
</p></div></dd><dt>2. </dt><dd><div id="hyperchol.TF.2.2"><p class="no_margin">Data on <i>LDLRAP1</i> variants is still relatively limited and may evolve over time.</p></div></dd></dl></div></div></div></div><div id="hyperchol.Penetrance"><h3>Penetrance</h3><p><b><i>APOB.</i></b> Penetrance for FH may be reduced in persons with a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heterozygous/">heterozygous</a> <i>APOB</i> variant [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.doi.2021.e018263">Doi et al 2021</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.kamar.2021.572045">Kamar et al 2021</a>].</p><p><b><i>LDLR.</i></b> Studies report that only 73% of individuals with a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heterozygous/">heterozygous</a> <i>LDLR</i> variant (especially partial <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/loss-of-function/">loss-of-function</a> variants) have an LDL-C level &#x0003e;130 mg/dL, suggesting lower <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/penetrance/">penetrance</a> than previously proposed [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.khera.2016.2578">Khera et al 2016</a>].</p><p>
<b>
<i>PCSK9</i>
</b>
</p><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Penetrance is approximately 90% in persons <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heterozygous/">heterozygous</a> for the <a href="/sites/books/NBK174884/table/hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_3/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobhypercholTfamilialhypercholesterolemi3">c.381T&#x0003e;A</a> (p.Ser127Arg) <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.dullaart.2017.1790">Dullaart 2017</a>].</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Penetrance in persons <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heterozygous/">heterozygous</a> for the <a href="/sites/books/NBK174884/table/hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_3/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobhypercholTfamilialhypercholesterolemi3">p.Asp374Tyr</a> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> is high, with FH manifesting at a young age [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.naoumova.2005.2654">Naoumova et al 2005</a>].</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Penetrance for other <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heterozygous/">heterozygous</a> <i>PCSK9</i> variants remains largely unknown [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.cariou.2011.258">Cariou et al 2011</a>].</div></li></ul></div><div id="hyperchol.Nomenclature"><h3>Nomenclature</h3><p>While the term "<a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/homozygous/">homozygous</a>" is generally used in genetics to denote the presence of the same <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> in both alleles of a given <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/gene/">gene</a>, the term "homozygous FH" or "HoFH" is used in the medical literature to denote the presence of any two pathogenic variants (<a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/compound-heterozygous/">compound heterozygous</a> or homozygous) on both alleles of <i>APOB</i>, <i>LDLR</i>, or <i>PCSK9</i>.</p><p>The term "<a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/autosomal-recessive/">autosomal recessive</a> FH" refers to FH caused by <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/biallelic/">biallelic</a> pathogenic variants in <i>LDLRAP1</i>.</p></div><div id="hyperchol.Prevalence"><h3>Prevalence</h3><p>The prevalence of HeFH in the general population was traditionally cited as 1:500; however, data suggest that it may be as high as ~1:250 [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.benn.2012.3956">Benn et al 2012</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.nordestgaard.2013.3478">Nordestgaard et al 2013</a>]. This prevalence has been primarily based on studies from Europe, North America, East Asia, and Australia. The prevalence of HeFH remains unknown in 90% of countries in the world, including countries in Africa, Asia, and South America [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.beheshti.2020.2553">Beheshti et al 2020</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.hu.2020.1742">Hu et al 2020</a>].</p><p>The prevalence of HoFH due to <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/biallelic/">biallelic</a> pathogenic variants in <i>APOB</i>, <i>LDLR</i>, or <i>PCSK9</i> is estimated at 1:160,000 to 1:400,000 [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.nordestgaard.2013.3478">Nordestgaard et al 2013</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.cuchel.2014.2146">Cuchel et al 2014</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.beheshti.2020.2553">Beheshti et al 2020</a>].</p><p>The prevalence of <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/autosomal-recessive/">autosomal recessive</a> FH due to <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/biallelic/">biallelic</a> pathogenic variants in <i>LDLRAP1</i> is lower than 1:1,000,000 [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.derasmo.2020.56">D'Erasmo et al 2020</a>]. An increased <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/carrier/">carrier</a> frequency of 1:143 in Sardinia, Italy, for autosomal recessive <i>LDLRAP1</i>-related FH is the result of founder variants (see <a href="/sites/books/NBK174884/table/hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_3/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobhypercholTfamilialhypercholesterolemi3">Table 11</a>).</p><p>FH is more common in several populations (<a href="/sites/books/NBK174884/table/hyperchol.T.prevalence_of_familial_hyper/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobhypercholTprevalenceoffamilialhyper">Table 3</a>) because of founder variants (see <a href="/sites/books/NBK174884/table/hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_3/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobhypercholTfamilialhypercholesterolemi3">Table 11</a>).</p><div id="hyperchol.T.prevalence_of_familial_hyper" class="table"><h3><span class="label">Table 3. </span></h3><div class="caption"><p>Prevalence of Familial Hypercholesterolemia in Select Populations</p></div><p class="large-table-link" style="display:none"><span class="right"><a href="/sites/books/NBK174884/table/hyperchol.T.prevalence_of_familial_hyper/?report=objectonly" target="object">View in own window</a></span></p><div class="large_tbl" id="__hyperchol.T.prevalence_of_familial_hyper_lrgtbl__"><table class="no_bottom_margin"><thead><tr><th id="hd_h_hyperchol.T.prevalence_of_familial_hyper_1_1_1_1" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Population</th><th id="hd_h_hyperchol.T.prevalence_of_familial_hyper_1_1_1_2" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Prevalence&#x000a0;<sup>1</sup></th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.prevalence_of_familial_hyper_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">General population</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.prevalence_of_familial_hyper_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">~1:250</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.prevalence_of_familial_hyper_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">French Canadian</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.prevalence_of_familial_hyper_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">1:80&#x000a0;<sup>2,&#x000a0;3</sup></td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.prevalence_of_familial_hyper_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Old Order Amish</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.prevalence_of_familial_hyper_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">1:10&#x000a0;<sup>4</sup></td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.prevalence_of_familial_hyper_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Christian Lebanese</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.prevalence_of_familial_hyper_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">1:85</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.prevalence_of_familial_hyper_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Tunisia</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.prevalence_of_familial_hyper_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">1:165</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.prevalence_of_familial_hyper_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">South African Afrikaner</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.prevalence_of_familial_hyper_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">1:72 to 1:100</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.prevalence_of_familial_hyper_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">South African Ashkenazi Jew</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.prevalence_of_familial_hyper_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">1:67</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.prevalence_of_familial_hyper_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Icelandic</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.prevalence_of_familial_hyper_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">1:836&#x000a0;<sup>3</sup></td></tr></tbody></table></div><div><div><dl class="temp-labeled-list small"><dt>1. </dt><dd><div id="hyperchol.TF.3.1"><p class="no_margin">
<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.austin.2004.407">Austin et al [2004]</a>
</p></div></dd><dt>2. </dt><dd><div id="hyperchol.TF.3.2"><p class="no_margin">A common &#x0003e;10-kb <i>LDLR</i> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/deletion/">deletion</a> that includes <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/exon/">exon</a> 1 (60% of alleles) and a ~5-kb <i>LDLR</i> deletion (5% of alleles) result in a significant increase in LDL-C [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.simard.2004.202">Simard et al 2004</a>].</p></div></dd><dt>3. </dt><dd><div id="hyperchol.TF.3.3"><p class="no_margin">
<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.brunham.2021.2629">Brunham &#x00026; Hegele [2021]</a>
</p></div></dd><dt>4. </dt><dd><div id="hyperchol.TF.3.4"><p class="no_margin">Amish individuals <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heterozygous/">heterozygous</a> for <i>APOB</i> variant <a href="/sites/books/NBK174884/table/hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_3/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobhypercholTfamilialhypercholesterolemi3">p.Arg3527Gln</a> (the most common <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> in the Amish community) have average LDL-C levels below the suggested minimum for a diagnosis of FH. However, coronary artery calcification and atherosclerosis still occur in heterozygous Amish individuals with lower average LDL-C levels [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.andersen.2016.443">Andersen et al 2016</a>].</p></div></dd></dl></div></div></div></div></div><div id="hyperchol.Genetically_Related_Allelic_Di"><h2 id="_hyperchol_Genetically_Related_Allelic_Di_">Genetically Related (Allelic) Disorders</h2><p>No phenotypes other than those discussed in this <i>GeneReview</i> are known to be associated with <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/germline/">germline</a> pathogenic variants in <i>LDLR</i> or <i>LDLRAP1</i>.</p><p>Other phenotypes associated with <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/germline/">germline</a> pathogenic variants in <i>APOB</i> and <i>PCSK9</i> are summarized in <a href="/sites/books/NBK174884/table/hyperchol.T.allelic_phenotypes/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobhypercholTallelicphenotypes">Table 4</a>.</p><div id="hyperchol.T.allelic_phenotypes" class="table"><h3><span class="label">Table 4. </span></h3><div class="caption"><p>Allelic Phenotypes</p></div><p class="large-table-link" style="display:none"><span class="right"><a href="/sites/books/NBK174884/table/hyperchol.T.allelic_phenotypes/?report=objectonly" target="object">View in own window</a></span></p><div class="large_tbl" id="__hyperchol.T.allelic_phenotypes_lrgtbl__"><table class="no_bottom_margin"><thead><tr><th id="hd_h_hyperchol.T.allelic_phenotypes_1_1_1_1" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Gene</th><th id="hd_h_hyperchol.T.allelic_phenotypes_1_1_1_2" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Phenotype</th><th id="hd_h_hyperchol.T.allelic_phenotypes_1_1_1_3" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Pathogenic Variants</th><th id="hd_h_hyperchol.T.allelic_phenotypes_1_1_1_4" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Clinical Characteristics</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.allelic_phenotypes_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>APOB</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.allelic_phenotypes_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<a href="/sites/books/n/gene/apob-hbl/"><i>APOB</i>-related familial hypobetalipoproteinemia</a>
</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.allelic_phenotypes_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Typically truncating <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/biallelic/">biallelic</a> or <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heterozygous/">heterozygous</a> variants</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.allelic_phenotypes_1_1_1_4" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Homozygous: hepatomegaly, steatorrhea, growth deficiency, deficiency of fat-soluble vitamins, GI, &#x00026; neurologic dysfunction; plasma total cholesterol, LDL-C, &#x00026; apo B levels typically &#x0003c;5% for age &#x00026; sex</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Heterozygous: possible risk of liver dysfunction &#x00026; hepatic steatosis; rarely (~5%-10% of persons), severe nonalcoholic steatosis &#x02192; cirrhosis</div></li></ul>
</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.allelic_phenotypes_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>PCSK9</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.allelic_phenotypes_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Hypocholesterolemia w/&#x02193; LDL cholesterol (OMIM <a href="https://omim.org/entry/603776" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">603776</a>)</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.allelic_phenotypes_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Loss-of-function variants</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.allelic_phenotypes_1_1_1_4" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">&#x02191; LDL receptors on surface of liver cells, &#x02191; removal of LDL-C from blood (&#x00026; therefore &#x02193; LDL-C levels) &#x00026; &#x02193; risk for CAD.</td></tr></tbody></table></div><div><div><dl class="temp-labeled-list small"><dt></dt><dd><div><p class="no_margin">CAD = coronary artery disease; GI = gastrointestinal</p></div></dd></dl></div></div></div></div><div id="hyperchol.Differential_Diagnosis"><h2 id="_hyperchol_Differential_Diagnosis_">Differential Diagnosis</h2><p>Genetic conditions with clinical and/or laboratory findings similar to those of <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/familial/">familial</a> hypercholesterolemia (FH) are summarized in <a href="/sites/books/NBK174884/table/hyperchol.T.genetic_disorders_with_featu/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobhypercholTgeneticdisorderswithfeatu">Table 5</a>.</p><div
