For a description of databases (Locus Specific, HGMD, ClinVar) to which links are provided, click
<a href="/sites/books/n/gene/app1/">here</a>.</p></div></dd></dl></div></div></div><div id="hyperchol.molgen.TB" class="table"><h3><span class="label">Table B.</span></h3><div class="caption"><p>OMIM Entries for Familial Hypercholesterolemia (<a href="/omim/107730,143890,144010,603776,603813,605747,606945,607786" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=entrez&amp;targetcat=term&amp;targettype=omim">View All in OMIM</a>) </p></div><p class="large-table-link" style="display:none"><span class="right"><a href="/sites/books/NBK174884/table/hyperchol.molgen.TB/?report=objectonly" target="object">View in own window</a></span></p><div class="large_tbl" id="__hyperchol.molgen.TB_lrgtbl__"><table><tbody><tr><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">
<a href="/omim/107730" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=entrez&amp;targetcat=term&amp;targettype=omim">107730</a></td><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">APOLIPOPROTEIN B; APOB</td></tr><tr><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">
<a href="/omim/143890" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=entrez&amp;targetcat=term&amp;targettype=omim">143890</a></td><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">HYPERCHOLESTEROLEMIA, FAMILIAL, 1; FHCL1</td></tr><tr><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">
<a href="/omim/144010" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=entrez&amp;targetcat=term&amp;targettype=omim">144010</a></td><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">HYPERCHOLESTEROLEMIA, FAMILIAL, 2; FHCL2</td></tr><tr><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">
<a href="/omim/603776" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=entrez&amp;targetcat=term&amp;targettype=omim">603776</a></td><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">HYPERCHOLESTEROLEMIA, FAMILIAL, 3; FHCL3</td></tr><tr><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">
<a href="/omim/603813" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=entrez&amp;targetcat=term&amp;targettype=omim">603813</a></td><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">HYPERCHOLESTEROLEMIA, FAMILIAL, 4; FHCL4</td></tr><tr><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">
<a href="/omim/605747" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=entrez&amp;targetcat=term&amp;targettype=omim">605747</a></td><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">LOW DENSITY LIPOPROTEIN RECEPTOR ADAPTOR PROTEIN 1; LDLRAP1</td></tr><tr><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">
<a href="/omim/606945" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=entrez&amp;targetcat=term&amp;targettype=omim">606945</a></td><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">LOW DENSITY LIPOPROTEIN RECEPTOR; LDLR</td></tr><tr><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">
<a href="/omim/607786" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=entrez&amp;targetcat=term&amp;targettype=omim">607786</a></td><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">PROPROTEIN CONVERTASE, SUBTILISIN/KEXIN-TYPE, 9; PCSK9</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><div id="hyperchol.Molecular_Pathogenesis"><h3>Molecular Pathogenesis</h3><p><i>LDLR</i> encodes low-density lipoprotein receptor (LDLR), a cell surface protein involved in endocytosis of low-density lipoprotein cholesterol (LDL-C). After LDL-C is bound at the cell membrane, it is taken into the cell and to lysosomes where the protein moiety is degraded and the cholesterol molecule suppresses cholesterol synthesis via negative feedback. Pathogenic variants in <i>LDLR</i> usually either reduce the number of receptors produced within the cells or disrupt the ability of the receptor to bind LDL-C, resulting in high levels of plasma LDL-C.</p><p><i>APOB</i> encodes apolipoprotein B-100. <i>APOB</i> pathogenic variants alter the ability of apolipoprotein B-100 to effectively bind LDL-C to LDLR on the cell surface, causing fewer LDL-C particles to be removed from the blood.</p><p>The PCSK9 protein product binds to LDLRs and promotes their degradation in intracellular acidic compartments. Pathogenic variants in <i>PCSK9</i> have been associated both with hypercholesterolemia and hypocholesterolemia. Gain-of-function pathogenic variants cause hypercholesterolemia by excessive degradation of LDLRs, reducing the amount of LDL-C removed from the blood. Loss-of-function pathogenic variants cause hypocholesterolemia (reduced blood cholesterol levels) by increasing the number of LDLRs on the surface of liver cells, resulting in a quicker-than-usual removal of LDL-C from the blood and reduced incidence of coronary artery disease [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.cohen.2006.1264">Cohen et al 2006</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.pandit.2008.1333">Pandit et al 2008</a>].</p><p>The LDLRAP1 protein plays an important role in moving LDLRs and their attached LDL particles into the liver cells for recycling. Biallelic <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/loss-of-function/">loss-of-function</a> <i>LDLRAP1</i> variants have been the primary disease mechanism reported and inhibit the removal of LDL-C from the blood [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.derasmo.2020.56">D'Erasmo et al 2020</a>].</p><div id="hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_1" class="table"><h3><span class="label">Table 9. </span></h3><div class="caption"><p>Familial Hypercholesterolemia: Mechanism of Disease Causation</p></div><p class="large-table-link" style="display:none"><span class="right"><a href="/sites/books/NBK174884/table/hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_1/?report=objectonly" target="object">View in own window</a></span></p><div class="large_tbl" id="__hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_1_lrgtbl__"><table class="no_bottom_margin"><thead><tr><th id="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_1_1_1_1_1" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Gene&#x000a0;<sup>1</sup></th><th id="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_1_1_1_1_2" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Mechanism of Disease Causation</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_1_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>APOB</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_1_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Loss-of-function variants that typically impair LDL-C binding to the LDLR</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_1_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>LDLR</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_1_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Loss of function</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_1_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>LDLRAP1</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_1_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Loss of function; however, may change over time, as data on variants in this <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/gene/">gene</a> are currently limited.</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_1_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>PCSK9</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_1_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Gain of function</td></tr></tbody></table></div><div><div><dl class="temp-labeled-list small"><dt>1. </dt><dd><div id="hyperchol.TF.9.1"><p class="no_margin">Genes from <a href="/sites/books/NBK174884/table/hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobhypercholTmoleculargenetictestingus">Table 1</a> in alphabetic order</p></div></dd></dl></div></div></div><div id="hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_2" class="table"><h3><span class="label">Table 10. </span></h3><div class="caption"><p>Familial Hypercholesterolemia: Gene-Specific Laboratory Considerations</p></div><p class="large-table-link" style="display:none"><span class="right"><a href="/sites/books/NBK174884/table/hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_2/?report=objectonly" target="object">View in own window</a></span></p><div class="large_tbl" id="__hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_2_lrgtbl__"><table class="no_bottom_margin"><thead><tr><th id="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_2_1_1_1_1" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Gene&#x000a0;<sup>1</sup></th><th id="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_2_1_1_1_2" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Special Consideration</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_2_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>APOB</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_2_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Intron distribution w/in <i>APOB</i> is unusual: 24 of the 29 introns occur in the 5' terminus. &#x0003e;50% of the protein is encoded by 7,572-bp <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/exon/">exon</a> 26, 1 of the largest exons reported in the human genome. 1 of the 2 main <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/isoforms/">isoforms</a>, apolipoprotein B-100, synthesized exclusively in the liver, is responsible for complications related to FH [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.whitfield.2004.1725">Whitfield et al 2004</a>].</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_2_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>LDLR</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_2_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Exon 4 encodes LDLR type A repeats 3, 4, &#x00026; 5, located in the ligand-binding <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/domain/">domain</a>. This region is considered a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/hot-spot/">hot spot</a> &#x00026; has been reported to have the most disease-causing variants per nucleotide. Exons 2-8 &#x00026; <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/exon/">exon</a> 14 contain a total of 60 highly conserved cysteine residues that are vital for proper protein folding &#x00026; function [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.chora.2022.293">Chora et al 2022</a>].</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_2_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>PCSK9</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_hyperchol.T.familial_hypercholesterolemi_2_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"><i>PCSK9</i> pathogenic variants are assoc w/both hypercholesterolemia &#x00026; hypocholesterolemia. Gain-of-function pathogenic variants cause hypercholesterolemia by excessive degradation of LDLRs, &#x02193;ing the amount of LDL-C removed from blood. Loss-of-function pathogenic variants cause hypocholesterolemia by &#x02191;ing the number of LDLRs on the surface of liver cells, resulting in &#x02191;ed removal of LDL-C from blood &#x00026; &#x02193;ed incidence of coronary artery disease [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.cohen.2006.1264">Cohen et al 2006</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.pandit.2008.1333">Pandit et al 2008</a>].</td></tr></tbody></table></div><div><div><dl class="temp-labeled-list small"><dt>1. </dt><dd><div id="hyperchol.TF.10.1"><p class="no_margin">Genes from <a href="/sites/books/NBK174884/table/hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobhypercholTmoleculargenetictestingus">Table 1</a> in alphabetic GeneReviews staff have not independently verified the classification of variants.</p></div></dd><dt></dt><dd><div><p class="no_margin">GeneReviews follows the standard naming conventions of the Human Genome Variation Society (<a href="https://varnomen.hgvs.org/" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">varnomen<wbr style="display:inline-block"></wbr>​.hgvs.org</a>). See <a href="/sites/books/n/gene/app3/">Quick Reference</a> for an explanation of nomenclature.</p></div></dd><dt>1. </dt><dd><div id="hyperchol.TF.11.1"><p class="no_margin">Genes from <a href="/sites/books/NBK174884/table/hyperchol.T.molecular_genetic_testing_us/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobhypercholTmoleculargenetictestingus">Table 1</a> in alphabetic order</p></div></dd><dt>2. </dt><dd><div id="hyperchol.TF.11.2"><p class="no_margin">Variant designation that does not conform to current naming conventions. In this case, numbering is based on mature peptide before cleavage of signal peptide and corresponding nucleotides.</p></div></dd><dt>3. </dt><dd><div id="hyperchol.TF.11.3"><p class="no_margin">
<a href="https://uswest.ensembl.org/Homo_sapiens/Variation/Summary?r=2:21228660-21229660;toggle_HGVS_names=open;v=rs5742904;vdb=variation;vf=3917324" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">rs5742904</a>
</p></div></dd></dl></div></div></div></div></div><div id="hyperchol.Chapter_Notes"><h2 id="_hyperchol_Chapter_Notes_">Chapter Notes</h2><div id="hyperchol.Author_Notes"><h3>Author Notes</h3><p><a href="https://med.stanford.edu/knowleslab.html" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">Dr Knowles</a> is a physician-scientist, and the overall theme of his research is the genetic basis of cardiovascular disease, with contributions across the continuum from discovery to the development of model systems to the translation of these findings to the clinic and most recently to the public health aspect of genetics. Currently his discovery and basic translational efforts center on understanding the genetic basis of insulin resistance using GWAS studies coupled with exploration in model systems including standard cell lines, induced pluripotent stem cells and murine models. These efforts are funded through the NIH. His clinical translational focus is on the intersection of lipids and insulin resistance, particularly defining how statins increase the risk of diabetes (Doris Duke Foundation grant). His clinical role at Stanford is as a lipidologist and director of the Familial Hypercholesterolemia (FH) clinic, where they provide world-class care to individuals with FH and related lipid disorders. His interest in FH has led him to explore innovative ways of addressing the major public health impact of this genetic condition. As the volunteer Chief Medical Advisor of the FH Foundation (a patient-led research and advocacy organization), he led the FH Foundation's efforts to establish a national patient registry for FH (CASCADE FH) and apply for an ICD10 code for FH. He has used cutting-edge "big-data" approaches to identify previously undiagnosed FH patients in electronic medical records (the FIND FH project, partly funded by the AHA).</p><p><a href="https://med.stanford.edu/knowleslab/group-members.html" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">Hannah Ison</a> is a licensed Cardiovascular Genetic Counselor at the Stanford Center for Inherited Cardiovascular Disease. In her role she primarily specializes in caring for patients and families with inherited dyslipidemias and has been actively involved in efforts to identify and care for individuals at risk for <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/familial/">familial</a> hypercholesterolemia (FH) for close to five years. She works with both adult and pediatric patients and has assisted with and managed the development of the Pediatric Lipid Clinic at Stanford Children's Hospital. Additionally, she is a member of the National Society of Genetic Counselors and is co-chair of the Dyslipidemia Working Group through this organization.</p><p><a href="https://profiles.stanford.edu/shoa" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">Shoa Clarke</a> is a preventive cardiologist and a geneticist. He is boarded in internal medicine, pediatrics, and cardiovascular medicine. He cares for adults, children, and families with genetic risk factors for cardiovascular disease, including <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/familial/">familial</a> hypercholesterolemia.</p></div><div id="hyperchol.Acknowledgments"><h3>Acknowledgments</h3><p>The FH Foundation, AHA, NIH, Doris Duke Foundation</p></div><div id="hyperchol.Author_History"><h3>Author History</h3><p>Shoa L Clarke, MD, PhD (2022-present)<br />Hannah E Ison, MS, LCGC (2022-present)<br />Joshua W Knowles, MD, PhD (2014-present)<br />Mitchel Pariani, MS, LCGC; Stanford Center for Inherited Cardiovascular Disease (2016-2022)<br />Emily Youngblom, PhD, MPH; University of Washington School of Public Health (2014-2022)</p></div><div id="hyperchol.Revision_History"><h3>Revision History</h3><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>30 January 2025 (sw) Revision: long-term safety and efficacy of evinacumab in HoFH [<a class="bk_pop" href="#hyperchol.REF.gaudet.2024.2422">Gaudet et al 2024</a>]</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>7 July 2022 (sw) Comprehensive update posted live</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>8 December 2016 (sw) Comprehensive update posted live</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>2 January 2014 (me) Review posted live</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>5 August 2013 (jl) Original submission</div></li></ul></div></div><div id="hyperchol.References"><h2 id="_hyperchol_References_">References</h2><div id="hyperchol.Published_Guidelines__Consensu"><h3>Published Guidelines / Consensus Statements</h3><ul class="simple-list"><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Chora JR, Iacocca MA, Tich&#x000fd; L, Wand H, Kurtz CL, Zimmermann H, Leon A, Williams M, Humphries SE, Hooper AJ, Trinder M, Brunham LR, Costa Pereira A, Jannes CE, Chen M, Chonis J, Wang J, Kim S, Johnston T, Soucek P, Kramarek M, Leigh SE, Carri&#x000e9; A, Sijbrands EJ, Hegele RA, Freiberger T, Knowles JW, Bourbon M, et al. <script type="text/javascript">
    (function($){
        $('.skiplink').each(function(i, item){
            var href = $($(item).attr('href'));
            href.attr('tabindex', '-1').addClass('skiptarget'); // ensure the target can receive focus
            $(item).on('click', function(event){
                event.preventDefault();
                $.scrollTo(href, 0, {
                    onAfter: function(){
                        href.focus();
                    }
                });
            });
        });
    })(jQuery);
</script>
